I mean… I work out because the job kind of demands it, you know? Like if you can’t haul gear or climb stairs with a pack on, people notice, and honestly people depend on you. So there’s always that voice in my head about being ready.

And I keep telling myself it’s discipline, like when we do drills and everyone’s trying to be the guy who doesn’t complain, but… yeah. It’s probably gone past that.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight or what you’ve eaten?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about that kind of stuff in a general way, like guys at the station joke about “cutting weight” or whatever, especially when someone’s training for a race or trying to pass a fitness thing, and it’s all very… macho and stupid sometimes.\n\nBut as far as, like, making myself sick or taking something after I eat… I don’t really have a clear answer beyond, I don’t think that’s the thing I’m doing. My problem is more that I get in my head before I eat, and then I just… keep it tight, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively or push yourself harder physically to make up for what you’ve eaten or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I work out because the job kind of demands it, you know? Like if you can’t haul gear or climb stairs with a pack on, people notice, and honestly people depend on you. So there’s always that voice in my head about being ready.\n\nBut, uh… whether I do it to “make up” for eating, I’m not sure. Sometimes after a bigger meal I feel like I should move around or do something productive, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. It’s hard to tell because at the station everybody’s always doing something — lifting, running, talking about macros, some guy training for a charity stair climb… so it kind of blends in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the way you look has been affecting how you feel about yourself, like feeling like you’re not good enough or not where you should be, even if others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t love admitting that, but yeah. Like before work I’ll catch myself checking in the mirror, not even in a normal “do I look presentable” way. It’s more like… is my waist tight enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look like I’ve got it together.\n\nAnd if I don’t think I do, it kind of throws me off. I’ll still go do the job, obviously, but I feel less… I don’t know, less ready to be taken seriously. Which is dumb, because nobody’s probably looking at me that closely — they’re worried about the rig, the call, their coffee order, whatever. Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I’m not missing calls or screwing up reports or anything like that. The work part, when the bell hits, you just kind of go.

But the station stuff… that’s changed a little. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate — which, I mean, sometimes there’s always something to clean in a firehouse, so it doesn’t look that weird.

It’s just… I used to be more in the middle of that, joking around, eating with everyone. Now I keep to myself more when food’s involved, and I guess that makes me less part of the team than I used to be. I don’t like saying that, but yeah, it’s affecting that part.

It’s more like… is my waist tight enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look like I’ve got it together.\n\nAnd if I don’t think I do, it kind of throws me off. I’ll still go do the job, obviously, but I feel less… I don’t know, less ready to be taken seriously. Which is dumb, because nobody’s probably looking at me that closely — they’re worried about the rig, the call, their coffee order, whatever. But in my head it starts to feel like I’m not command-ready that day, like I’m not where I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve felt like you’ve lost control over how much you’re eating, where you end up eating a lot in a short amount of time, even if you didn’t plan to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Not like that, anyway. I mean, there are shifts where food is just weird — like somebody brings in a tray of something or we’re between calls and everyone’s standing around eating fast because the tones could drop any second. So sometimes it’s like, you eat kind of quickly and then you’re like, “Why did I inhale that?”\n\nBut that feels more like the job rhythm than me losing control, if that makes sense. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe too aware. So… no, I can’t really think of times where I eat a lot and feel like I couldn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about food, weight, or your appearance been affecting your ability to focus at work, interact with others, or enjoy activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I’m not missing calls or screwing up reports or anything like that. The work part, when the bell hits, you just kind of go.\n\nBut the station stuff… that’s changed a little. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate — which, I mean, sometimes there’s always something to clean in a firehouse, so it doesn’t look that weird.\n\nIt’s just… I used to be more in the middle of that, joking around, eating with everyone. Now I keep to myself more when food’s involved, and I guess that makes me less part of the team than I used to be. I don’t like saying that, but yeah, it’s affecting that part.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of the fire station, have your concerns about food, weight, or your appearance affected other areas of your life, like spending time with family, friends, or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, a little. I mean, not like I’ve stopped my whole life or anything. I still show up, still do what I need to do.

It’s more obvious at the station, honestly. Like when the crew orders food or everyone’s sitting down together, I’ll kind of find a reason to be somewhere else. Check equipment, wipe down the bay, whatever. There’s always some little task you can make look important in a firehouse — half the time somebody’s lost a tool or the coffee pot needs cleaning, you know?

But yeah… I guess the real answer is I’m keeping to myself more when food’s involved. I used to be more in the mix with everybody, joking around, eating with the crew. Now I duck out, and that probably makes me less part of the team than I was.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I would say it’s more tied to that than I want to admit.

Like before work, I’ll catch myself checking the mirror — not even the whole “do I look okay” thing, but my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t like what I see, it kind of follows me into the shift. Which is stupid, because nobody on scene cares what your face looks like when you’re pulling hose or forcing a door, you know?

But in my head, if I look… softer or off somehow, I don’t feel as confident. Like I’m less ready, less command-ready. And then I’m thinking about that instead of just getting into the day. So yeah, I think it probably does affect how I feel about myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t know what counts as “significant,” exactly. I haven’t been, like, trying to make some big announcement about it or anything.

But my gear and my station clothes fit different now. They kind of hang looser, especially around the waist. At first I told myself that was just, you know, better conditioning or maybe the uniforms stretching out — those station pants never fit right anyway. We’re always complaining about whoever orders them.

But then during the fitness review, they flagged that my weight was low for my build. And I heard that, I did. It’s just… part of me still thinks maybe I could trim down a little more, even though I know that probably doesn’t sound right.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… mostly, yeah. I mean, at first I told myself it was just eating cleaner, you know? Like if I’m going to go for the lieutenant track, I should look squared away, be disciplined, be the guy who’s not grabbing whatever’s in the kitchen at 2 a.m.

And then it kind of… slid. Now some days it’s black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner, even on shift days where I know full well we ran calls and I burned way more than that. I’ll still tell myself I’m just being “clean” or staying sharp, but if I’m honest, it’s tied up with wanting my body to look a certain way. Like leaner, more under control.

I mean, I’ve thought about that kind of stuff in a general way, like guys at the station joke about “cutting weight” or whatever, especially when someone’s training for a race or trying to pass a fitness thing, and it’s all very… macho and stupid sometimes.\n\nBut as far as, like, making myself sick or taking something after I eat… I don’t really have a clear answer beyond, I don’t think that’s the thing I’m doing. My problem is more that I get in my head before I eat, and then I just… keep it tight, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively or push yourself harder physically to make up for what you’ve eaten or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I work out because the job kind of demands it, you know? Like if you can’t haul gear or climb stairs with a pack on, people notice, and honestly people depend on you. So there’s always that voice in my head about being ready.\n\nBut, uh… whether I do it to “make up” for eating, I’m not sure. Sometimes after a bigger meal I feel like I should move around or do something productive, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. It’s hard to tell because at the station everybody’s always doing something — lifting, running, talking about macros, some guy training for a charity stair climb… so it kind of blends in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the way you look has been affecting how you feel about yourself, like feeling like you’re not good enough or not where you should be, even if others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t love admitting that, but yeah. okay” thing, but my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t like what I see, it kind of follows me into the shift. Which is stupid, because nobody on scene cares what your face looks like when you’re pulling hose or forcing a door, you know?\n\nBut in my head, if I look… softer or off somehow, I don’t feel as confident. So yeah, I think it probably does affect how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, or do you feel that your weight is much lower than it should be because of how you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know what counts as “significant,” exactly. At first I told myself that was just, you know, better conditioning or maybe the uniforms stretching out — those station pants never fit right anyway. We’re always complaining about whoever orders them.\n\nBut then during the fitness review, they flagged that my weight was low for my build. And I heard that, I did. It’s just… part of me still thinks maybe I could trim down a little more, even though I know that probably doesn’t sound right.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict your eating or choose certain foods, is it mainly because you're trying to change your weight or the way your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. I mean, at first I told myself it was just eating cleaner, you know? Like if I’m going to go for the lieutenant track, I should look squared away, be disciplined, be the guy who’s not grabbing whatever’s in the kitchen at 2 a.m.\n\nAnd then it kind of… slid. Now some days it’s black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner, even on shift days where I know full well we ran calls and I burned way more than that. I’ll still tell myself I’m just being “clean” or staying sharp, but if I’m honest, it’s tied up with wanting my body to look a certain way. Like leaner, more under control.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your weight or the shape of your body has been influencing how you judge yourself, like feeling like you've done better on a day when you've eaten less or lost a little more weight?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it does, honestly.

Like, before work I’ll look in the mirror and it’s not just, “Do I look professional?” It’s more like I’m checking my waist, or whether my face looks sharp enough. Which sounds weird saying out loud, because at the station there’s a million more important things going on — half the time someone’s yelling about coffee or the rig inventory or whatever.

But if I look and I feel like I look… off, or softer, it kind of gets in my head. I don’t feel as confident that day. Like I’m not as ready or as… command-ready, I guess. So yeah, I end up judging myself based on that more than I probably should.

Can you tell me what’s been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track — I’m a firefighter, so there’s always that pressure to be squared away physically. \n\nBut now it’s like… I’ll go through a whole shift on black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner, even when I know I’ve burned way more than that. And I keep telling myself it’s discipline, like when we do drills and everyone’s trying to be the guy who doesn’t complain, but… yeah. It’s probably gone past that.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight or what you’ve eaten?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about that kind of stuff in a general way, like guys at the station joke about “cutting weight” or whatever, especially when someone’s training for a race or trying to pass a fitness thing, and it’s all very… macho and stupid sometimes.\n\nBut as far as, like, making myself sick or taking something after I eat… I don’t really have a clear answer beyond, I don’t think that’s the thing I’m doing. My problem is more that I get in my head before I eat, and then I just… keep it tight, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively or push yourself harder physically to make up for what you’ve eaten or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I work out because the job kind of demands it, you know? Like if you can’t haul gear or climb stairs with a pack on, people notice, and honestly people depend on you. So there’s always that voice in my head about being ready.\n\nBut, uh… whether I do it to “make up” for eating, I’m not sure. Sometimes after a bigger meal I feel like I should move around or do something productive, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. It’s hard to tell because at the station everybody’s always doing something — lifting, running, talking about macros, some guy training for a charity stair climb… so it kind of blends in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the way you look has been affecting how you feel about yourself, like feeling like you’re not good enough or not where you should be, even if others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t love admitting that, but yeah. Like before work I’ll catch myself checking in the mirror, not even in a normal “do I look presentable” way. It’s more like… is my waist tight enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look like I’ve got it together.\n\nAnd if I don’t think I do, it kind of throws me off. I’ll still go do the job, obviously, but I feel less… I don’t know, less ready to be taken seriously. Which is dumb, because nobody’s probably looking at me that closely — they’re worried about the rig, the call, their coffee order, whatever. But in my head it starts to feel like I’m not command-ready that day, like I’m not where I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve felt like you’ve lost control over how much you’re eating, where you end up eating a lot in a short amount of time, even if you didn’t plan to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Not like that, anyway. I mean, there are shifts where food is just weird — like somebody brings in a tray of something or we’re between calls and everyone’s standing around eating fast because the tones could drop any second. So sometimes it’s like, you eat kind of quickly and then you’re like, “Why did I inhale that?”\n\nBut that feels more like the job rhythm than me losing control, if that makes sense. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe too aware. So… no, I can’t really think of times where I eat a lot and feel like I couldn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about food, weight, or your appearance been affecting your ability to focus at work, interact with others, or enjoy activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I’m not missing calls or screwing up reports or anything like that. The work part, when the bell hits, you just kind of go.\n\nBut the station stuff… that’s changed a little. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate — which, I mean, sometimes there’s always something to clean in a firehouse, so it doesn’t look that weird.\n\nIt’s just… I used to be more in the middle of that, joking around, eating with everyone. Now I keep to myself more when food’s involved, and I guess that makes me less part of the team than I used to be. I don’t like saying that, but yeah, it’s affecting that part.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the fire station, have your concerns about food, weight, or your appearance affected other areas of your life, like spending time with family, friends, or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, not like I’ve stopped my whole life or anything. I still show up, still do what I need to do.\n\nIt’s more obvious at the station, honestly. Like when the crew orders food or everyone’s sitting down together, I’ll kind of find a reason to be somewhere else. Check equipment, wipe down the bay, whatever. There’s always some little task you can make look important in a firehouse — half the time somebody’s lost a tool or the coffee pot needs cleaning, you know?\n\nBut yeah… I guess the real answer is I’m keeping to myself more when food’s involved. I used to be more in the mix with everybody, joking around, eating with the crew. Now I duck out, and that probably makes me less part of the team than I was.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your self-worth is strongly tied to your weight or the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I would say it’s more tied to that than I want to admit.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself checking the mirror — not even the whole “do I look okay” thing, but my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t like what I see, it kind of follows me into the shift. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Especially at the station, because meals there are kind of a whole production, you know? Somebody makes a huge pan of pasta or burgers or whatever, and everybody’s joking around, going back for seconds, arguing about the game or the dishwasher being loaded wrong.

And I’ll be sitting there trying to act normal, but I’m already doing the math in my head. If I eat like everyone else, I start worrying about what that means later. Like if I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound — even if it’s probably just water or a big meal — I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it.

It’s not even totally rational. I know nobody’s standing there checking my weight. But it feels like people are going to see me as soft, or like I don’t have control. So yeah, meals where everyone else is eating freely… they mess with my head more than I like to admit.

It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track — I’m a firefighter, so there’s always that pressure to be squared away physically. \n\nBut now it’s like… I’ll go through a whole shift on black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner, even when I know I’ve burned way more than that. And I keep telling myself it’s discipline, like when we do drills and everyone’s trying to be the guy who doesn’t complain, but… yeah. It’s probably gone past that.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight or what you’ve eaten?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about that kind of stuff in a general way, like guys at the station joke about “cutting weight” or whatever, especially when someone’s training for a race or trying to pass a fitness thing, and it’s all very… macho and stupid sometimes.\n\nBut as far as, like, making myself sick or taking something after I eat… I don’t really have a clear answer beyond, I don’t think that’s the thing I’m doing. My problem is more that I get in my head before I eat, and then I just… keep it tight, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively or push yourself harder physically to make up for what you’ve eaten or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I work out because the job kind of demands it, you know? Like if you can’t haul gear or climb stairs with a pack on, people notice, and honestly people depend on you. So there’s always that voice in my head about being ready.\n\nBut, uh… whether I do it to “make up” for eating, I’m not sure. Sometimes after a bigger meal I feel like I should move around or do something productive, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. It’s hard to tell because at the station everybody’s always doing something — lifting, running, talking about macros, some guy training for a charity stair climb… so it kind of blends in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the way you look has been affecting how you feel about yourself, like feeling like you’re not good enough or not where you should be, even if others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t love admitting that, but yeah. Like before work I’ll catch myself checking in the mirror, not even in a normal “do I look presentable” way. It’s more like… is my waist tight enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look like I’ve got it together.\n\nAnd if I don’t think I do, it kind of throws me off. I’ll still go do the job, obviously, but I feel less… I don’t know, less ready to be taken seriously. Which is dumb, because nobody’s probably looking at me that closely — they’re worried about the rig, the call, their coffee order, whatever. But in my head it starts to feel like I’m not command-ready that day, like I’m not where I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve felt like you’ve lost control over how much you’re eating, where you end up eating a lot in a short amount of time, even if you didn’t plan to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Not like that, anyway. I mean, there are shifts where food is just weird — like somebody brings in a tray of something or we’re between calls and everyone’s standing around eating fast because the tones could drop any second. So sometimes it’s like, you eat kind of quickly and then you’re like, “Why did I inhale that?”\n\nBut that feels more like the job rhythm than me losing control, if that makes sense. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe too aware. So… no, I can’t really think of times where I eat a lot and feel like I couldn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about food, weight, or your appearance been affecting your ability to focus at work, interact with others, or enjoy activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I’m not missing calls or screwing up reports or anything like that. The work part, when the bell hits, you just kind of go.\n\nBut the station stuff… that’s changed a little. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate — which, I mean, sometimes there’s always something to clean in a firehouse, so it doesn’t look that weird.\n\nIt’s just… I used to be more in the middle of that, joking around, eating with everyone. Now I keep to myself more when food’s involved, and I guess that makes me less part of the team than I used to be. I don’t like saying that, but yeah, it’s affecting that part.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the fire station, have your concerns about food, weight, or your appearance affected other areas of your life, like spending time with family, friends, or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, not like I’ve stopped my whole life or anything. I still show up, still do what I need to do.\n\nIt’s more obvious at the station, honestly. Like when the crew orders food or everyone’s sitting down together, I’ll kind of find a reason to be somewhere else. Check equipment, wipe down the bay, whatever. There’s always some little task you can make look important in a firehouse — half the time somebody’s lost a tool or the coffee pot needs cleaning, you know?\n\nBut yeah… I guess the real answer is I’m keeping to myself more when food’s involved. I used to be more in the mix with everybody, joking around, eating with the crew. Now I duck out, and that probably makes me less part of the team than I was.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your self-worth is strongly tied to your weight or the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I would say it’s more tied to that than I want to admit.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself checking the mirror — not even the whole “do I look okay” thing, but my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t like what I see, it kind of follows me into the shift. Which is stupid, because nobody on scene cares what your face looks like when you’re pulling hose or forcing a door, you know?\n\nBut in my head, if I look… softer or off somehow, I don’t feel as confident. Like I’m less ready, less command-ready. And then I’m thinking about that instead of just getting into the day. So yeah, I think it probably does affect how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, or do you feel that your weight is much lower than it should be because of how you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know what counts as “significant,” exactly. I haven’t been, like, trying to make some big announcement about it or anything.\n\nBut my gear and my station clothes fit different now. They kind of hang looser, especially around the waist. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe too aware. So… no, I can’t really think of times where I eat a lot and feel like I couldn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about food, weight, or your appearance been affecting your ability to focus at work, interact with others, or enjoy activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I’m not missing calls or screwing up reports or anything like that. The work part, when the bell hits, you just kind of go.\n\nBut the station stuff… that’s changed a little. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate — which, I mean, sometimes there’s always something to clean in a firehouse, so it doesn’t look that weird.\n\nIt’s just… I used to be more in the middle of that, joking around, eating with everyone. Now I keep to myself more when food’s involved, and I guess that makes me less part of the team than I used to be. I don’t like saying that, but yeah, it’s affecting that part.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the fire station, have your concerns about food, weight, or your appearance affected other areas of your life, like spending time with family, friends, or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, not like I’ve stopped my whole life or anything. I still show up, still do what I need to do.\n\nIt’s more obvious at the station, honestly. Like when the crew orders food or everyone’s sitting down together, I’ll kind of find a reason to be somewhere else. Check equipment, wipe down the bay, whatever. There’s always some little task you can make look important in a firehouse — half the time somebody’s lost a tool or the coffee pot needs cleaning, you know?\n\nBut yeah… I guess the real answer is I’m keeping to myself more when food’s involved. I used to be more in the mix with everybody, joking around, eating with the crew. Now I duck out, and that probably makes me less part of the team than I was.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your self-worth is strongly tied to your weight or the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I would say it’s more tied to that than I want to admit.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself checking the mirror — not even the whole “do I look okay” thing, but my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t like what I see, it kind of follows me into the shift. Which is stupid, because nobody on scene cares what your face looks like when you’re pulling hose or forcing a door, you know?\n\nBut in my head, if I look… softer or off somehow, I don’t feel as confident. Like I’m less ready, less command-ready. And then I’m thinking about that instead of just getting into the day. So yeah, I think it probably does affect how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, or do you feel that your weight is much lower than it should be because of how you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know what counts as “significant,” exactly. I haven’t been, like, trying to make some big announcement about it or anything.\n\nBut my gear and my station clothes fit different now. They kind of hang looser, especially around the waist. At first I told myself that was just, you know, better conditioning or maybe the uniforms stretching out — those station pants never fit right anyway. We’re always complaining about whoever orders them.\n\nBut then during the fitness review, they flagged that my weight was low for my build. And I heard that, I did. It’s just… part of me still thinks maybe I could trim down a little more, even though I know that probably doesn’t sound right.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict your eating or choose certain foods, is it mainly because you're trying to change your weight or the way your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. I mean, at first I told myself it was just eating cleaner, you know? Like if I’m going to go for the lieutenant track, I should look squared away, be disciplined, be the guy who’s not grabbing whatever’s in the kitchen at 2 a.m.\n\nAnd then it kind of… slid. Now some days it’s black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner, even on shift days where I know full well we ran calls and I burned way more than that. I’ll still tell myself I’m just being “clean” or staying sharp, but if I’m honest, it’s tied up with wanting my body to look a certain way. Like leaner, more under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body has been influencing how you judge yourself, like feeling like you've done better on a day when you've eaten less or lost a little more weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, honestly.\n\nLike, before work I’ll look in the mirror and it’s not just, “Do I look professional?” It’s more like I’m checking my waist, or whether my face looks sharp enough. Which sounds weird saying out loud, because at the station there’s a million more important things going on — half the time someone’s yelling about coffee or the rig inventory or whatever.\n\nBut if I look and I feel like I look… off, or softer, it kind of gets in my head. I don’t feel as confident that day. Like I’m not as ready or as… command-ready, I guess. So yeah, I end up judging myself based on that more than I probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed around mealtimes or when you're in situations where others are eating more freely?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Especially at the station, because meals there are kind of a whole production, you know? Somebody makes a huge pan of pasta or burgers or whatever, and everybody’s joking around, going back for seconds, arguing about the game or the dishwasher being loaded wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there trying to act normal, but I’m already doing the math in my head. If I eat like everyone else, I start worrying about what that means later. Like if I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound — even if it’s probably just water or a big meal — I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it.\n\nIt’s not even totally rational. I know nobody’s standing there checking my weight. But it feels like people are going to see me as soft, or like I don’t have control. So yeah, meals where everyone else is eating freely… they mess with my head more than I like to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah… I mean, I think I already said it, but yes. It kind of becomes this whole gauge for me.

So there’s always that voice in my head about being ready.\n\nBut, uh… whether I do it to “make up” for eating, I’m not sure. Sometimes after a bigger meal I feel like I should move around or do something productive, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. It’s hard to tell because at the station everybody’s always doing something — lifting, running, talking about macros, some guy training for a charity stair climb… so it kind of blends in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the way you look has been affecting how you feel about yourself, like feeling like you’re not good enough or not where you should be, even if others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t love admitting that, but yeah. Like before work I’ll catch myself checking in the mirror, not even in a normal “do I look presentable” way. It’s more like… is my waist tight enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look like I’ve got it together.\n\nAnd if I don’t think I do, it kind of throws me off. I’ll still go do the job, obviously, but I feel less… I don’t know, less ready to be taken seriously. Which is dumb, because nobody’s probably looking at me that closely — they’re worried about the rig, the call, their coffee order, whatever. But in my head it starts to feel like I’m not command-ready that day, like I’m not where I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve felt like you’ve lost control over how much you’re eating, where you end up eating a lot in a short amount of time, even if you didn’t plan to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Not like that, anyway. I mean, there are shifts where food is just weird — like somebody brings in a tray of something or we’re between calls and everyone’s standing around eating fast because the tones could drop any second. So sometimes it’s like, you eat kind of quickly and then you’re like, “Why did I inhale that?”\n\nBut that feels more like the job rhythm than me losing control, if that makes sense. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe too aware. So… no, I can’t really think of times where I eat a lot and feel like I couldn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about food, weight, or your appearance been affecting your ability to focus at work, interact with others, or enjoy activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I’m not missing calls or screwing up reports or anything like that. The work part, when the bell hits, you just kind of go.\n\nBut the station stuff… that’s changed a little. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate — which, I mean, sometimes there’s always something to clean in a firehouse, so it doesn’t look that weird.\n\nIt’s just… I used to be more in the middle of that, joking around, eating with everyone. Now I keep to myself more when food’s involved, and I guess that makes me less part of the team than I used to be. I don’t like saying that, but yeah, it’s affecting that part.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the fire station, have your concerns about food, weight, or your appearance affected other areas of your life, like spending time with family, friends, or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, not like I’ve stopped my whole life or anything. I still show up, still do what I need to do.\n\nIt’s more obvious at the station, honestly. Like when the crew orders food or everyone’s sitting down together, I’ll kind of find a reason to be somewhere else. Check equipment, wipe down the bay, whatever. There’s always some little task you can make look important in a firehouse — half the time somebody’s lost a tool or the coffee pot needs cleaning, you know?\n\nBut yeah… I guess the real answer is I’m keeping to myself more when food’s involved. I used to be more in the mix with everybody, joking around, eating with the crew. Now I duck out, and that probably makes me less part of the team than I was.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your self-worth is strongly tied to your weight or the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I would say it’s more tied to that than I want to admit.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself checking the mirror — not even the whole “do I look okay” thing, but my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t like what I see, it kind of follows me into the shift. Which is stupid, because nobody on scene cares what your face looks like when you’re pulling hose or forcing a door, you know?\n\nBut in my head, if I look… softer or off somehow, I don’t feel as confident. Like I’m less ready, less command-ready. And then I’m thinking about that instead of just getting into the day. So yeah, I think it probably does affect how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, or do you feel that your weight is much lower than it should be because of how you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know what counts as “significant,” exactly. I haven’t been, like, trying to make some big announcement about it or anything.\n\nBut my gear and my station clothes fit different now. They kind of hang looser, especially around the waist. At first I told myself that was just, you know, better conditioning or maybe the uniforms stretching out — those station pants never fit right anyway. We’re always complaining about whoever orders them.\n\nBut then during the fitness review, they flagged that my weight was low for my build. And I heard that, I did. It’s just… part of me still thinks maybe I could trim down a little more, even though I know that probably doesn’t sound right.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict your eating or choose certain foods, is it mainly because you're trying to change your weight or the way your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. I mean, at first I told myself it was just eating cleaner, you know? Like if I’m going to go for the lieutenant track, I should look squared away, be disciplined, be the guy who’s not grabbing whatever’s in the kitchen at 2 a.m.\n\nAnd then it kind of… slid. Now some days it’s black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner, even on shift days where I know full well we ran calls and I burned way more than that. I’ll still tell myself I’m just being “clean” or staying sharp, but if I’m honest, it’s tied up with wanting my body to look a certain way. Like leaner, more under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body has been influencing how you judge yourself, like feeling like you've done better on a day when you've eaten less or lost a little more weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, honestly.\n\nLike, before work I’ll look in the mirror and it’s not just, “Do I look professional?” It’s more like I’m checking my waist, or whether my face looks sharp enough. Which sounds weird saying out loud, because at the station there’s a million more important things going on — half the time someone’s yelling about coffee or the rig inventory or whatever.\n\nBut if I look and I feel like I look… off, or softer, it kind of gets in my head. I don’t feel as confident that day. Like I’m not as ready or as… command-ready, I guess. So yeah, I end up judging myself based on that more than I probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed around mealtimes or when you're in situations where others are eating more freely?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Especially at the station, because meals there are kind of a whole production, you know? Somebody makes a huge pan of pasta or burgers or whatever, and everybody’s joking around, going back for seconds, arguing about the game or the dishwasher being loaded wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there trying to act normal, but I’m already doing the math in my head. If I eat like everyone else, I start worrying about what that means later. Like if I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound — even if it’s probably just water or a big meal — I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it.\n\nIt’s not even totally rational. I know nobody’s standing there checking my weight. But it feels like people are going to see me as soft, or like I don’t have control. So yeah, meals where everyone else is eating freely… they mess with my head more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body influences how you judge yourself overall, like feeling better about who you are on days when you’ve eaten less or lost weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… overall, yeah. I mean, it sounds kind of shallow when I say it, but before work I’ll catch myself in the mirror checking my waist or whether my face looks, like, sharp enough.\n\nAnd if it doesn’t, it really throws me off. I start feeling less confident, like I’m not as squared away or command-ready that day. Which is stupid, because nobody on the crew is probably thinking about that — they’re thinking about the call sheet or whether the probie restocked the fridge — but in my head it becomes this whole measure of how I’m doing as a person.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body influences how you judge yourself overall, like feeling better about who you are on days when you’ve eaten less or lost weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think I already said it, but yes. It kind of becomes this whole gauge for me.\n\nLike before shift, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not just making sure my uniform looks okay. I’ll be looking at my waist, or my face, whether it looks sharp enough. And if it doesn’t, it sticks with me. I’ll feel less confident, less like I’m ready to be in charge or handle things the way I should.\n\nWhich is dumb, because the guys are usually worried about the truck or breakfast or someone leaving gear all over the bay, not my face. But in my head, if I don’t look right, I feel like I’m not doing right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Anorexia Nervosa, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed around mealtimes or when you're in situations where others are eating more freely?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Especially at the station, because meals there are kind of a whole production, you know? Somebody makes a huge pan of pasta or burgers or whatever, and everybody’s joking around, going back for seconds, arguing about the game or the dishwasher being loaded wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there trying to act normal, but I’m already doing the math in my head. If I eat like everyone else, I start worrying about what that means later. Like if I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound — even if it’s probably just water or a big meal — I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it.\n\nIt’s not even totally rational. I start feeling less confident, like I’m not as squared away or command-ready that day. Which is stupid, because nobody on the crew is probably thinking about that — they’re thinking about the call sheet or whether the probie restocked the fridge — but in my head it becomes this whole measure of how I’m doing as a person.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body influences how you judge yourself overall, like feeling better about who you are on days when you’ve eaten less or lost weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think I already said it, but yes. It kind of becomes this whole gauge for me.\n\nLike before shift, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not just making sure my uniform looks okay. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Even if everything else is fine — like the truck’s stocked, the shift’s going smooth, nobody’s on my case — if I catch myself in the mirror before work and my waist looks… I don’t know, softer, or my face doesn’t look sharp enough, it kind of tanks my confidence.

And then I’m thinking about that while I’m getting ready, which is ridiculous because half the time we’re just arguing over who drank the last coffee or whether someone moved the nozzle bag. But it still gets in my head. I feel less put-together, less like I’m ready to lead or handle the day right.

Can you tell me what’s been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track — I’m a firefighter, so there’s always that pressure to be squared away physically. \n\nBut now it’s like… I’ll go through a whole shift on black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner, even when I know I’ve burned way more than that. And I keep telling myself it’s discipline, like when we do drills and everyone’s trying to be the guy who doesn’t complain, but… yeah. It’s probably gone past that.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight or what you’ve eaten?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about that kind of stuff in a general way, like guys at the station joke about “cutting weight” or whatever, especially when someone’s training for a race or trying to pass a fitness thing, and it’s all very… macho and stupid sometimes.\n\nBut as far as, like, making myself sick or taking something after I eat… I don’t really have a clear answer beyond, I don’t think that’s the thing I’m doing. My problem is more that I get in my head before I eat, and then I just… keep it tight, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively or push yourself harder physically to make up for what you’ve eaten or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I work out because the job kind of demands it, you know? Like if you can’t haul gear or climb stairs with a pack on, people notice, and honestly people depend on you. So there’s always that voice in my head about being ready.\n\nBut, uh… whether I do it to “make up” for eating, I’m not sure. Sometimes after a bigger meal I feel like I should move around or do something productive, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. It’s hard to tell because at the station everybody’s always doing something — lifting, running, talking about macros, some guy training for a charity stair climb… so it kind of blends in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the way you look has been affecting how you feel about yourself, like feeling like you’re not good enough or not where you should be, even if others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t love admitting that, but yeah. Like before work I’ll catch myself checking in the mirror, not even in a normal “do I look presentable” way. It’s more like… is my waist tight enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look like I’ve got it together.\n\nAnd if I don’t think I do, it kind of throws me off. I’ll still go do the job, obviously, but I feel less… I don’t know, less ready to be taken seriously. Which is dumb, because nobody’s probably looking at me that closely — they’re worried about the rig, the call, their coffee order, whatever. But in my head it starts to feel like I’m not command-ready that day, like I’m not where I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve felt like you’ve lost control over how much you’re eating, where you end up eating a lot in a short amount of time, even if you didn’t plan to?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Not like that, anyway. I mean, there are shifts where food is just weird — like somebody brings in a tray of something or we’re between calls and everyone’s standing around eating fast because the tones could drop any second. So sometimes it’s like, you eat kind of quickly and then you’re like, “Why did I inhale that?”\n\nBut that feels more like the job rhythm than me losing control, if that makes sense. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe too aware. So… no, I can’t really think of times where I eat a lot and feel like I couldn’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about food, weight, or your appearance been affecting your ability to focus at work, interact with others, or enjoy activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I’m not missing calls or screwing up reports or anything like that. The work part, when the bell hits, you just kind of go.\n\nBut the station stuff… that’s changed a little. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate — which, I mean, sometimes there’s always something to clean in a firehouse, so it doesn’t look that weird.\n\nIt’s just… I used to be more in the middle of that, joking around, eating with everyone. Now I keep to myself more when food’s involved, and I guess that makes me less part of the team than I used to be. I don’t like saying that, but yeah, it’s affecting that part.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the fire station, have your concerns about food, weight, or your appearance affected other areas of your life, like spending time with family, friends, or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, not like I’ve stopped my whole life or anything. I still show up, still do what I need to do.\n\nIt’s more obvious at the station, honestly. Like when the crew orders food or everyone’s sitting down together, I’ll kind of find a reason to be somewhere else. Check equipment, wipe down the bay, whatever. There’s always some little task you can make look important in a firehouse — half the time somebody’s lost a tool or the coffee pot needs cleaning, you know?\n\nBut yeah… I guess the real answer is I’m keeping to myself more when food’s involved. I used to be more in the mix with everybody, joking around, eating with the crew. Now I duck out, and that probably makes me less part of the team than I was.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your self-worth is strongly tied to your weight or the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I would say it’s more tied to that than I want to admit.\n\nLike before work, I’ll catch myself checking the mirror — not even the whole “do I look okay” thing, but my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And if I don’t like what I see, it kind of follows me into the shift. Which is stupid, because nobody on scene cares what your face looks like when you’re pulling hose or forcing a door, you know?\n\nBut in my head, if I look… softer or off somehow, I don’t feel as confident. Like I’m less ready, less command-ready. And then I’m thinking about that instead of just getting into the day. So yeah, I think it probably does affect how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, or do you feel that your weight is much lower than it should be because of how you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know what counts as “significant,” exactly. I haven’t been, like, trying to make some big announcement about it or anything.\n\nBut my gear and my station clothes fit different now. They kind of hang looser, especially around the waist. At first I told myself that was just, you know, better conditioning or maybe the uniforms stretching out — those station pants never fit right anyway. We’re always complaining about whoever orders them.\n\nBut then during the fitness review, they flagged that my weight was low for my build. And I heard that, I did. It’s just… part of me still thinks maybe I could trim down a little more, even though I know that probably doesn’t sound right.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict your eating or choose certain foods, is it mainly because you're trying to change your weight or the way your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. I mean, at first I told myself it was just eating cleaner, you know? Like if I’m going to go for the lieutenant track, I should look squared away, be disciplined, be the guy who’s not grabbing whatever’s in the kitchen at 2 a.m.\n\nAnd then it kind of… slid. Now some days it’s black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner, even on shift days where I know full well we ran calls and I burned way more than that. I’ll still tell myself I’m just being “clean” or staying sharp, but if I’m honest, it’s tied up with wanting my body to look a certain way. Like leaner, more under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body has been influencing how you judge yourself, like feeling like you've done better on a day when you've eaten less or lost a little more weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, honestly.\n\nLike, before work I’ll look in the mirror and it’s not just, “Do I look professional?” It’s more like I’m checking my waist, or whether my face looks sharp enough. Which sounds weird saying out loud, because at the station there’s a million more important things going on — half the time someone’s yelling about coffee or the rig inventory or whatever.\n\nBut if I look and I feel like I look… off, or softer, it kind of gets in my head. I don’t feel as confident that day. Like I’m not as ready or as… command-ready, I guess. So yeah, I end up judging myself based on that more than I probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed around mealtimes or when you're in situations where others are eating more freely?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Especially at the station, because meals there are kind of a whole production, you know? Somebody makes a huge pan of pasta or burgers or whatever, and everybody’s joking around, going back for seconds, arguing about the game or the dishwasher being loaded wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there trying to act normal, but I’m already doing the math in my head. If I eat like everyone else, I start worrying about what that means later. Like if I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound — even if it’s probably just water or a big meal — I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it.\n\nIt’s not even totally rational. I know nobody’s standing there checking my weight. But it feels like people are going to see me as soft, or like I don’t have control. Like leaner, more under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body has been influencing how you judge yourself, like feeling like you've done better on a day when you've eaten less or lost a little more weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, honestly.\n\nLike, before work I’ll look in the mirror and it’s not just, “Do I look professional?” It’s more like I’m checking my waist, or whether my face looks sharp enough. Which sounds weird saying out loud, because at the station there’s a million more important things going on — half the time someone’s yelling about coffee or the rig inventory or whatever.\n\nBut if I look and I feel like I look… off, or softer, it kind of gets in my head. I don’t feel as confident that day. Like I’m not as ready or as… command-ready, I guess. So yeah, I end up judging myself based on that more than I probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed around mealtimes or when you're in situations where others are eating more freely?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Especially at the station, because meals there are kind of a whole production, you know? Somebody makes a huge pan of pasta or burgers or whatever, and everybody’s joking around, going back for seconds, arguing about the game or the dishwasher being loaded wrong.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there trying to act normal, but I’m already doing the math in my head. If I eat like everyone else, I start worrying about what that means later. Like if I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound — even if it’s probably just water or a big meal — I get this tight panic in my chest, like I’ve blown it.\n\nIt’s not even totally rational. I know nobody’s standing there checking my weight. But it feels like people are going to see me as soft, or like I don’t have control. So yeah, meals where everyone else is eating freely… they mess with my head more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body influences how you judge yourself overall, like feeling better about who you are on days when you’ve eaten less or lost weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… overall, yeah. I mean, it sounds kind of shallow when I say it, but before work I’ll catch myself in the mirror checking my waist or whether my face looks, like, sharp enough.\n\nAnd if it doesn’t, it really throws me off. I start feeling less confident, like I’m not as squared away or command-ready that day. Which is stupid, because nobody on the crew is probably thinking about that — they’re thinking about the call sheet or whether the probie restocked the fridge — but in my head it becomes this whole measure of how I’m doing as a person.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or the shape of your body influences how you judge yourself overall, like feeling better about who you are on days when you’ve eaten less or lost weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think I already said it, but yes. It kind of becomes this whole gauge for me.\n\nLike before shift, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not just making sure my uniform looks okay. I’ll be looking at my waist, or my face, whether it looks sharp enough. And if it doesn’t, it sticks with me. I’ll feel less confident, less like I’m ready to be in charge or handle things the way I should.\n\nWhich is dumb, because the guys are usually worried about the truck or breakfast or someone leaving gear all over the bay, not my face. But in my head, if I don’t look right, I feel like I’m not doing right.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself more harshly on days when your weight or shape feels off, even if other parts of your life are going well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even if everything else is fine — like the truck’s stocked, the shift’s going smooth, nobody’s on my case — if I catch myself in the mirror before work and my waist looks… I don’t know, softer, or my face doesn’t look sharp enough, it kind of tanks my confidence.\n\nAnd then I’m thinking about that while I’m getting ready, which is ridiculous because half the time we’re just arguing over who drank the last coffee or whether someone moved the nozzle bag. But it still gets in my head. I feel less put-together, less like I’m ready to lead or handle the day right."
